- AUG 19 1997 
CHARLES APPRENDI, APPELLATE DIVISION 
JR., (Filed Aug. 19, 1999) 


Defendant-Appellant. 


Argued February 4, 1997 - Decided AUG 19 1997 
Before Judges Stern, Humphreys and Wecker. 


On appeal from the Superior Court of New Jer- 
sey, Law Division, Cumberland County. 


Joseph D. O’Neill argued the cause for appellant 
(Joseph D. O’Neill, attorney; Mr. O’Neill and 
Charles I. Coant, on the brief). 


Lisa Sarnoff Gochman argued the cause for 
respondent (Peter Verniero, attorney General of 
New Jersey, attorney; Ms. Gochman, of counsel 
and on the brief). 


The opinion of the court was delivered by 
HUMPHREYS, J.A.D. 


The defendant appeals his extended term sentence 
which was imposed pursuant to N.J.S.A. 2C:44-3(e) (the 
“Hate Crime Statute”). The defendant asserts that the 
Statute is unconstitutional because: (1) the statute is too 
vague; (2) the statute violates his constitutional right to 


82a 


due process by using a preponderance of the evidence 
standard. 


We have thoroughly reviewed the record and consid- 
ered the arguments presented. We conclude that the stat- 
ute is constitutional and affirm. 


I 


The defendant fired a rifle on two occasions in 1994 
at the home of a neighbor. On the first occasion, a bullet 
entered the third floor bedroom of one of the neighbor's 
three children. On the second occasion, defendant fired 
several times and as a result the neighbor’s front door 
and windows were “bullet-riddled.” Fortunately, no one 
was injured. The defendant is white; the neighbor is an 
African-American. The police later searched the defen- 
dant’s home and found an “arsenal of weapons,” includ- 
ing an anti-personnel bomb. 


The defendant was charged in an indictment with 
various crimes, including first-degree attempted murder. 
He pled guilty on July 24, 1995 to counts three and 
eighteen charging him with the second degree crime of 
possession of a firearm for an unlawful purpose in viola- 
tion of N.J.S.A. 2C:39-4(a) and to count twenty-two charg- 
ing him with the third degree crime of unlawful 
possession of a prohibited weapon, the anti-personnel 
bomb, in violation of N.J.S.A. 2C:39-3(a). The defendant 
conditioned his plea upon the right to appeal his sen- 
tence. See R. 3:19-3(f). 


83a 


The State moved to have the defendant sentenced to 


an extended term pursuant to N.J.S.A. 2C:44-3(e). That 
statute provides: 


The court shall, upon application of the pros- 
ecuting attorney, sentence a person who has 
been convicted of a crime, . . . to an extended 


term if it finds, by a preponderance of the evi- 
dence, the grounds in subsection e. . . . 


e. The defendant in committing the crime 
acted, at least in part, with ill will, hatred or bias 
toward, and with a purpose to intimidate, an 
individual or group of individuals because of 


race, color, religion, sexual orientation or eth- 
nicity. 


[Ibid. but see L. 1995, c. 211, 93, eff. Aug. 14, 1995 
(amending the text of this statute).] 


The extended sentence for the defendant’s second 
degree crimes is between ten and twenty years which is 
the ordinary range for a first degree crime. For his third 
degree crime, the extended sentence would be five to ten 
years which is the ordinary range for a second degree 
crime. See N.J.S.A. 2C:43-7(a)(3), (4); N.J.S.A. 


2C:43-6(a)(2), (3). In effect, hate crimes are sentenced one 
degree higher. 


A police officer testified at the hearing on the State’s 
motion that the defendant had told the police that he shot 
at the house because he knew that blacks were living 
there and he wanted to give them “a message that they 
were in his neighborhood.” 


The defendant, a pharmacist, testified at the hearing. 
He said that he shot at the front door because the “glass 
and the purple door” caught his eye and that at the time 


84a 


of the incident, he was under the influence of alcohol and 
drugs. He denied that he shot at the house because he 
wanted to keep blacks away from the area. He also 
denied that he was a racist or a member of any racist 
group. He said that he had black friends and had no bias 
against African-Americans. He testified that he lied to the 
police about his reasons for shooting, because the police 
officer threatened him and the defendant wanted to get 
the interrogation over with. 


A psychologist testifying for the defendant at the 
hearing said that the defendant suffered from an obses- 
sive-compulsive disorder, a cyclothymic disorder (exces- 
sive mood swings), drug dependence, alcohol abuse, the 
impulse disorder of kleptomania, and premature ejacula- 
tion. The psychologist concluded that a person of defen- 
dant’s personality type would say or do almost anything, 
including lie, to get out of a police interrogation. 


The judge found that the testimony of the police 
officer was “credible, believable.” The judge said that the 
questioning of the defendant by the police “was not 
overbearing. It was not onerous or is not in any sense 
dictatorial.” The judge said he was “satisfied that the 
crime was motivated by racial bias” and that the defen- 
dant’s explanation for the shooting was bizarre. The 
judge concluded that the defendant had changed his 
story in order to avoid punishment for a bias crime. The 
judge found that “the standard of proof required by the 
statute [preponderance of the evidence] has been met. 
The defendant is subject to enhanced penalties.” 


The defendant was sentenced on count eighteen to an 
extended term of twelve years with a parole ineligibility 


85a 


of four years, the mandatory minimum parole inel- 
igibility under the Graves Act. See N.J.S.A. 2C:43-6(c). He 
was sentenced on count three to a concurrent term of 
seven years imprisonment with a three year parole bar 
and on count twenty-two to a concurrent term of three 
years imprisonment. He was ordered to pay $1,980 resti- 
tution to the victim. On each of the three counts, a $100 
Violent Crimes Compensation Board penalty and a $75 


Safe Street Act penalty were imposed. The remaining 
counts were dismissed. 


Il 


The defendant contends that the statute is too vague. 
See State v. Afanador, 134 N.J. 162, 170 (1993) (a criminal 
statute is unconstitutionally vague “if persons of common 
intelligence must necessarily guess at its meaning and 
differ as to its application”); State v. Cameron, 100 N.J. 586 
593 (1985) (a statute is facially invalid where it is “imper- 
missibly vague in all its application, that is, there is no 
conduct that it proscribes with sufficient certainty”). 


The defendant's contention is refuted by the decision 
of the New Jersey Supreme Court in State v. Mortimer, 135 
N.J. 517, 529, cert. denied, 513 U.S. 970, 115 S. Ct. 440, 130 
L. Ed. 2d 351 (1994). In that case, the Court considered the 
constitutionality of a section of the Hate Crime Statute, 
N.J.S.A. 2C:33-4(d), which provided that “harassment,” a 
petty disorderly persons offense, would be raised to a 
fourth degree offense if the defendant “acted, at least in 
part, with ill will, hatred or bias toward, and with a 
purpose to intimidate, an individual or group of individ- 
uals because of race, color, religion, sexual orientation or 


86a 


ethnicity.” The defendant in Mortimer, supra, challenged 
the statute on a number of constitutional grounds includ- 
ing vagueness. The Supreme Court said that if the statute 
were “construed narrowly and ~pplied to a proper predi- 
cate offense, subsection d wiihstunds a ‘vagueness’ chal- 
lenge.” 135 N.J. at 531. The Court cured the vagueness 
problem by excising the words “at least in part, with ill 
will, hatred or bias toward.” Id. at 534. 


The section of the Hate Crime Statute at issue in the 
present case, N.J.S.A. 2C:44-3(e), has the same language 
as that construed in Mortimer. As was done in Mortimer, 
we will excise the words “at least in part, with ill will, 
hatred or bias toward” and thereby overcome the vague- 
ness challenge. We note that the Legislature has already 
made this change to the text of the statute (the statute 
was amended by L.1995, c. 211, § 3, eff. Aug. 14, 1995). 


Ill 


The Hate Crime Statute provides for an extended 
term based upon a finding of bias by a preponderance of 
the evidence. N.J.S.A. 2C:44-3(e). The Due Process clause 
of the United States Constitution “protects the accused 
against conviction except upon proof beyond a reason- 
able doubt of every fact necessary to constitute the crime 
with which he is charged.” In re Winship, 397 U.S. 358, 
364, 90 S. Ct. 1068, 1073, 25 L. Ed. 2d 368, 375 (1970); 
accord State v. Anderson, 127 N.J. 191, 200-01 (1992). The 
defendant argues that using the preponderance of evi- 
dence standard to mandate an extended term sentence 
violates the constitutional requirement that the State 


87a 


must prove the elements of a crime by the reasonable 
doubt standard. 


The flaw in the defendant’s argument is that the 
Legislature has not made racial bias an element of the 
crime. The Hate Crime Statute acts as a sentencing factor. 
The language in the statute is not included in the defini- 
tion of any crime in the New Jersey Code of Criminal 
Justice. Instead, the statute is included in a section of the 
code entitled “authority of court in sentencing.” As to 
sentencing factors which are not elements of a crime, the 
State’s burden of proof is not subject to the reasonable 
doubt standard. See McMillan v. Pennsylvania, 477 U.S. 79, 
91, 106 S. Ct. 2411, 2419, 91 L. Ed. 2d 67, 79 (1986). 


A State has the power to define the elements of a 
crime. The State’s definition of the elements of the crime 
is usually “dispositive.” Id. at 85, 106 S. Ct. at 2415, 91 L. 
Ed. 2d at 75. The State’s power to define the elements of a 
crime has constitutional limits. Id. at 91, 106 S. Ct. at 2419, 
91 L. Ed. 2d at 79; Paterson v. New York, 432 U.S. 197, 210, 
97 S. Ct. 2319, 2327, 53 L. Ed. 2d 281, 292 (1977) (there are 
constitutional limits to the State’s power to redefine the 
elements of a crime so as to eliminate the reasonable 
doubt standard); Mullaney v. Wilbur, 421 U.S. 684, 699, 95 
S. Ct. 1881, 1889-90, 44 L. Ed. 2d 508, 519-20 (1975) (the 
State may not require a defendant charged with murder 
to bear the burden of proving the elements of manslaugh- 
ter in order to reduce the crime to manslaughter; the State 
may not undermine due process by redefining elements 
that constitute different crimes and characterizing them 
as factors bearing solely on punishment). 


88a 


In McMillan, supra, the State statute provided for a 
mandatory minimum sentence of five years imprison- 
ment if the sentencing judge found by a preponderance of 
the evidence that the defendant “visibly possessed” a 
firearm during the commission of certain enumerated 
felonies. The Supreme Court found the statute to be con- 
stitutional. The Court said that the constitutionality of a 
statute redefining a crime would “depend on differences 
of degree” and that the statute in McMillan fell on the 
“permissible side of the constitutional line.” 477 U.S. at 
91, 106 S. Ct. at 2419, 91 L. Ed. 2d at 79. 


The Court pointed out in McMillan that the statute 
did not create a presumption that the person was guilty 
of a crime and did not restructure existing crimes to 
evade the requirements of the Due Process clause. Id. at 
87, 106 S. Ct. at 2416-17, 91 L. Ed. 2d at 77. The Court said: 
“(t]he statute gives no impression of having been tailored 
to permit the visible possession finding to be a tail which 
wags the dog of the substantive offense.” Id. at 88, 106 S. 
Ct. at 2417, 91 L. Ed. 2d at 77. Instead, the State has taken 
a traditional sentencing factor, possession of a firearm, 
and “dictated the precise weight to be given that factor.” 
Id. at 89-90, 106 S. Ct. at 2418, 91 L. Ed. 2d at 79. The Court 
also said that the defendants’ claim that visible posses- 
sion is “really” an element of the crime, “would have at 
least more superficial appeal if a finding of visible pos- 
session exposed them to greater or additional punish- 
ment... .” Id. at 88, 106 S. Ct. at 2417, 91 L. Ed. 2d at 
77-78. 


The New Jersey Hate Crime Statute does not create a 
presumption of guilt nor does it restructure any crimes. 
The statute is not “tailored to permit the [racial or other 


89a 


bias] finding to be a tail which wags the dog of the 
substantive offense.” See id. at 88, 106 S. Ct. at 2417, 91 L. 
Ed. 2d at 77; State v. Federico, 198 N.J. Super. 120, 128-29 
(App. Div. 1984) (the elements distinguishing the degrees 
of the crime of kidnapping must be determined by the 
jury and not the judge to avoid undermining a defen- 
dant’s constitutional due process rights), aff'd, 103 N./. 
169 (1986) (affirming on statutory construction grounds; 
the Court expressly did not determine whether the result 
is “compelled by either the United States or New Jersey 
Constitutions”); see also State v. Anderson, 127 N.J. 191 
(1992) (provision in the Code of Criminal Justice that 
materiality in a prosecution for perjury is a matter of law 
for the judge to determine is a violation of the New Jersey 
Constitution because materiality is an element of the 
crime). 


Further, in New Jersey “motive traditionally has been 
an important factor for judges to consider at sentencing.” 
Mortimer, supra, 135 N.J. at 528. Here, as in McMillan, the 
State has taken a sentencing factor, the motive for the 
crime, and prescribed the weight to be given it. See 477 
U.S. at 89-90, 106 S. Ct. at 2418, 91 L. Ed. 2d at 79. 


Numerous cases have held that sentencing factors do 
not have to be established beyond a reasonable doubt. See 
State v. Biegenwald, 106 N.J. 13, 59 (1987) (ordinarily, the 
State’s contentions at a sentencing proceeding need not 
be proven beyond a reasonable doubt, even those that are 
statutorily prescribed); State v. Smith, 279 N.J. Super. 131, 
139 (App. Div. 1995) (the State need not prove beyond a 
reasonable doubt grading provisions which do not consti- 
tute elements of the offense); see also Graves Act, N.J.S.A. 
2C:43-6(c) (upon a judge’s finding by a preponderance of 


90a 


the evidence that a defendant used or possessed a gun 
while committing certain crimes, a sentence of three years 
without parole must be imposed); State v. Des Marets, 92 
N.J. 62 (1983) (the Graves Act does not unconstitutionally 
infringe upon the judicial power to suspend sentences); 
cf. Wisconsin v. Mitchell, 508 U.S. 476, 490, 113 S. Ct. 2194, 
2202, 124 L. Ed. 2d 436, 448 (1993) (a penalty enhancement 
statute for race-based selection of a victim was not 
unconstitutional, overbroad or violative of free speech); 
State v. Cotella, 298 N.J. Super. 668, 675-76, 690 A.2d 156 
(App. Div. 1997) (upholding an extended sentence 
imposed under the Hate Crime Statute). 


The New Jersey Hate Crime Statute does expose the 
defendant to “greater and additional punishment” and 
therefore the defendant’s claim has “superficial appeal.” 
See McMillan, supra, 477 U.S. at 88, 106 S. Ct. at 2417, 91 L. 
Ed. 2d at 78. However, this one factor standing alone does 
not place the statute on the “impermissible side of the 
constitutional line.” See id. at 91, 106 S. Ct. at 2419, 91 L. 
Ed. 2d at 79. Court decisions have consistently upheld the 
constitutionality of statutes containing sentencing factors 
which impose a greater or additional punishment. In 
United States v. Rumney, 867 F.2d 714, 717 (1st Cir.), cert. 
denied, 491 U.S. 908, 109 S. Ct. 3194, 105 L. Ed. 2d 702 
(1989), the court held that the heightened penalty pro- 
vided by the Armed Career Criminal Act (“ACCA”) 18 
U.S.C. § 924(e)(1), for persons who possess a firearm 
“after having been thrice convicted of burglary /robbery 
is a sentence enhancer [not] a substantive crime.” Vir- 
tually all of the other federal courts of appeal have 
reached the same conclusion. E.g., United States v. 
McGatha, 891 F.2d 1520, 1524 nn. 27, 29 (11th Cir.) (noting 


9la 


that all the federal circuit courts of appeal except the 
second and fifth circuit have held that the ACCA does not 
create a new offense but is merely a sentence enhance- 
ment provision), cert. denied, 495 U.S. 938, 110 S. Ct. 2188, 
109 L. Ed. 2d 516 (1990); but see United States v. Davis, 801 
F.2d 754 (Sth Cir. 1986); see also United States v. Palacios- 
Casquete, 55 F.3d 557 (11th Cir. 1995) (agreeing with four 
and disagreeing with one federal circuit courts of appeal 
by holding that a criminal statute regarding deported 
aliens remaining unlawfully in the United States is a 
sentence enhancing statute as opposed to a statute defin- 
ing a separate crime). 


The merits of hate crime statutes have been widely 
recognized. Most of the states have enacted hate crime 
statutes which provide for enhanced sentences for bias 
crimes. See Craig P. Gaumer, Punishment for Prejudice: A 
Commentary on the Constitutionality and Utility of State 
Statutory Responses to the Problem of Hate Crimes, 39 S. D. 
L. Rev. 1, 9 & n. 39 (1994) (noting that as of 1994, twenty- 
nine states had enacted Hate Crime Statutes which were 
“essentially” sentence enhancement statutes). 


In Mortimer, supra, the Supreme Court stated: 


[t]he Legislature can surely treat bias-motivated 
offenses with an enhanced level of severity, 
because bias crimes, by nature, have distinct 
harmful effects. As the Supreme Court has 
noted, “bias-motivated crimes are more likely 
[than other crimes] to provoke retaliatory 
crimes, inflict distinct emotional harms on their 
victims, and incite community unrest.” There- 
fore, a regulation providing harsher penalties 
for bias-motivated crimes is appropriate as a 


92a 


means of addressing the greater harm those 
crimes cause. 

Thus, we hold that subsection d does not violate 
the First Ame~dment. The statute neither regu- 
lates protectea expression nor impermissibly pun- 
ishes motive. 


[135 N.J. at 529 (citation omitted) (emphasis added).] 


In providing for enhanced sentencing in crimes moti- 
vated by racial bias, the Legislature has not trespassed on 
the fundamental element of criminal intent. The requisite 
criminal intent for this defendant's crimes has been estab- 
lished by his plea of guilty, namely, (1) having a firearm 
in his possession “with a purpose” to use it unlawfully 
against the person or property of another, N.J.S.A. 
2C:39-4(a); and (2) “knowingly” having in his possession 
a destructive device. N.J.S.A. 2C:39-3. Motive is quite 
different from intent. “ . . . [MJotive is the inducement for 
doing an act; the intent is the resolve to commit an act.” 
Stated differently, motive relates to the end; intent relates 
to the means. Bryan A. Garner A Dictionary of Modern 
Legal Usage 458 (2d Ed. 1995). 


In Morss v. Forbes, 24 N.J. 341, 359 (1957), the Court 
said that intent should not be “confused” with motive 
and “proof of motive is never essential to a conviction but 
may be evidential.” In Snakenberg v. Hartford Casualty Ins. 
Co., 383 S.E.2d 2,7 & n. 7 (S.C. Ct. App. 1989), the court 
said: “purpose relates to the result desired by the actor; 
motive is his subjective reason for desiring the result.” 
See also 21 Am. Jur. 2d Criminal Law § 173 (1981) (“In 
criminal law motive may be defined as that which leads 
or tempts the mind to indulge in a criminal act, or as the 
moving power which impels to action for a definite 


93a 


result. Motive is distinguishable from intent, which is the 
purpose to use a particular means to effect a certain 
result. Motive is not an essential element of any crime, 
unless made so by statute.”) 


The parties have not cited and our legal research has 
not uncovered any case in which the motive for the crime 
when used solely as a sentencing enhancer or factor is 
transformed into an element of the crime which then 
must be established beyond a reasonable doubt. The 
modern trend in the law is in the opposite direction. 
Sentencing judges are being given more discretion to 
engage in factfinding regarding the circumstances of the 
crime. As stated in a law review article: 


Under real-offense sentencing systems, [sen- 
tencing] judges are given spacious authority to 
determine an offender’s “real” crimes. . . . 


The United States Constitution, as currently 
interpreted, imposes virtually no restrictions on 
the practices of real-offense sentencing. Any 
constitutional constraints that might be imag- 
ined by lawyers or academics would require the 
making of “new Law,” and the present Supreme 
Court has given no signals of movement in that 
direction. Indeed, the few major rulings handed 
down by the Court Have uniformly given the 
states wide latitude in structuring their laws 
concerning factfinding at sentencing. 


[Kevin R. Reitz, Sentencing Facts: Travesties of 
Real-Offense Sentencing, 45 Stan. L. Rev. 523, 542 
(1993); see also Elizabeth T. Lear, Is Conviction 
Irrelevant?, 40 U.C.L.A. L. Rev. 1179 (1993) (crit- 
icizing real offense sentencing and McMillan); 
Susan N. Werman, The Tail that Wagged the Dog: 


94a 


Bifurcated Fact-Finding Under the Federal Sentenc- 
ing Guidelines and the Limits of Due Process, 66 S. 
Cal. L. Rev. 289 (1992) (same).] 


We recognize that in a given case, a draconian statu- 
tory sentencing mandate based upon a finding of motive 
reached only by a preponderance of the evidence could 
infringe on a defendant’s due process rights. However, 
we are also mindful that an act of the Legislature should 
not be lightly cast aside. Legislatures “are guardians of 
the liberty and welfare of the people in quite as great a 
degree as the courts.” Missouri, Kansas & Texas Railway Co. 
v. May, 194 U.S. 267, 270, 24 S. Ct. 638-39, 48 L.Ed. 971, 973 
(1904) (Holmes, J.). Further, “every possible presumption 
favors the validity of an act of the Legislature.” New 
Jersey Sports & Exposition Auth. v. McCrane, 61 N.J. 1, 8 
(1972). We are satisfied that this statute falls on the per- 
missible side of the constitutional line. See McMillan, 
supra, 477 U.S. at 91, 106 S. Ct. at 2419, 91 L. Ed. 2d at 79. 


In sum, if a person is convicted of a crime by a plea 
of guilty or by proof beyond a reasonable doubt, we find 
no constitutional impediment to a statutory requirement 
for an extended term upon the sentencing judge being 
satisfied by a preponderance of the evidence that the 
crime was committed because of racial hatred. 


Affirmed.

- MR. O’NEILL: Well, I do, Judge. 
THE COURT: Okay. 


MR. O’NEILL: If Your Honor pleases, the crite- 
tia for sentencing to an extended term under N.J.S.A. 
2C:44-3(e) is invalid because it’s based on proof required 
by the statute by a test or a standard known as by a 
Preponderance of the evidence. 


Now, we've briefed this, Judge. We’ve submitted 
three briefs. And the essence of the briefs is simply this; 


122a 


Colloquy 


[p. 27] that traditionally in the criminal law all essentials 
need to be proven by the State. Must be proven by not a 
preponderance of the evidence, as is indicated in the 
statute, but by the time-honored going through English 
and American history standard of proof beyond a reason- 
able doubt. 


Now, there is a case of State versus Des Marets on 92 
New Jersey, Page 62. That has to do with the Graves Act. 
And Your Honor is familiar with that case. And it says - 
written by Chief Justice Wilentz - that there’s only a 
showing required of possession of a firearm by a prepon- 
derance of the evidence. 


Now, that is very distinctfully (sic) different from our 
case here because, as Chief Justice Wilentz stated at Page 
69 of his opinion, that the statute itself strongly suggests 
that the actor’s state of mind was meant to be irrelevant. 
The quote, Judge, is - strongly suggests that the actor’s 
state of mind was meant to be irrelevant. 


Now, that’s very, very different from what confronts 
the Court here today. Because the mens rea or the guilty 
intent is absolutely required to be proven before the 
Court can determine whether or not a crime committed 
was racially biased or racially motivated. The specific 
intent of the defendant is required to be proven contrary 
to State versus Des Marets, where there was no intent 
required to be proven beyond a reasonable doubt because 
the actor’s state of mind, the [p. 28] defendant's state of 
mind, in the words of the Chief Justice, was meant to be 
irrelevant. 


123a 


Here, you can’t decide this case unless you can 
decide what was in the mind of this defendant at the time 
he committed those two crimes. And that has to be not by 
a mere preponderance of the evidence, not by clear and 
convincing evidence, but by the time-honored standard 
of proof beyond a reasonable doubt. 


That’s the difference here, Judge. It requires you to 
assess in this case the mental state, the intent of the 
defendant. Before you can determine whether the Pros- 
ecutor has proved what he must prove for the Court to 
sentence this man to an enhanced penalty, he’s got to 
prove beyond a reasonable doubt that there was racial 
motivation involved in the activities engaged in by this 
defendant. 


And I can go on and on and on. But I think as stated 
in our briefs, Judge, encapsulated, that’s what the posi- 
tion of the defense is as to the standard of proof. That 
statute is wrong. It’s invalid. It can’t be used in this case. 


MR. LUTHER: Judge, just briefly on the issue of 
whether or not the statute is constitutional. Again, I’ve 
already submitted a brief. But beyond a reasonable doubt 
only applies to elements of a crime. It does not apply to 
sentencing provisions. 


The Supreme Court in State versus Mortimer 
addressed [p. 29] identical language in the bias harass- 
ment statute and found it to be constitutional with certain 
language excised. And as detailed in my brief, the State 
respectfully submits that certain language be removed 
from the extended term statute in accordance with the 
Supreme Court's ruling in State versus Mortimer. As such, 
the statute would be constitutional. 


124a 


Would the Court also wish to hear argument at this 
time as to why an extended term should be granted? 


THE COURT: Certainly. 


MR. LUTHER: Okay. Judge, first let me just 
deal briefly with the defendant’s arguments. The defen- 
dant contends that he did not act out of racial bias. But, 
rather, says that he was drunk and under the influence of 


drugs. 


Now, first of all, Judge, this completely contradicts 
what he told the police. He told the police when he was 
arrested that he was not under the influence of drugs and 
that he was not drunk. He had taken some over-the- 
counter medication for his stomach, I believe. And he had 
taken — had one or two drinks at the Greek Island Restau- 
rant between 10:30 and midnight. That’s what he told the 
police that evening. He did not appear drunk. 


And then on September 5 of this year he testified 
under oath that he was under the influence of drugs and 
also drinking. However, he said that the last drugs he 
took that night were at 8 p.m. He spoke with the police at 
6 a.m. And [p. 30] he admitted that he acted out of racial 
bias. That’s ten hours after he took [t]he drugs. He admit- 
ted that his last drink was at 1:30 in the morning. That’s 
four-and-a-half hours before he spoke with the police. So 
I would submit that the evidence is clear that he was not 
drunk or under the influence of drugs. 


Also, Judge, you have to look at the purposeful and 
planned nature of his conduct. Judge, if I could just show 
the Court the rifle that Mr. Apprendi used that evening. It 
wasn’t just a regular rifle, Judge. He had a homemade 


125a 


silencer attached to the end of it. And not only did he 
have a homemade silencer, but he had a scope, as well. 
This scope is what he referred to as a toy in his testimony. 
He also called the silencer a toy when he testified before 
this Court. 


Judge, this is not just an ordinary scope on a rifle, it’s 
a laser scope. And if the Court would look at the wall 
over there, I’ve got the scope on right now. There’s a red 
laser beam which is on the wall of the court showing that 
this type of weapon is deadly accurate. And I would 
submit that this shows that this defendant was not drunk 
or under the influence of drugs when he was firing it 
repeatedly at the Fowlkes residence on December 22nd, 
1994. He knew exactly what he was doing. 


MS. FOWLKES: Amen. 


MR. LUTHER: He had the silencer on the gun 
to avoid apprehension. 


[p. 31] MR. FOWLKES: Uh-huh. 


MR. LUTHER: He had the scope on the gun to 
make sure that he hit his target. And hit his target, he did, 
repeatedly. Numerous shots going through the front door 
of the home, including hitting the black Santa Clause [sic] 
which was on that front door. 


Judge, the defendant says there was no racial 
animous (sic) here. He was just driving by the home and 
the purple door caught his attention. That’s what he tells 
the Court; okay? 


First of all, Judge, the door’s not purple; it’s bur- 
gundy. But even if the Court thinks burgundy is similar 
to purple, the more important point is that most of that 


126a 


door was glass. There was only a 6-inch wooden border 
around that door; okay? The door and the house sits 
a-hundred-and-eighty-feet from the roadway. So what 
was it that Mr. Apprendi saw on that door that caught his 
attention? Judge, it wasn’t the color purple. It was that 
black Santa Clause [sic] on the door that caught his 
attention. 


The defendant contends he doesn’t walk around 
making racist statements. Therefore, the State hasn't 
proven he acted with racial bias. Judge, most racists don’t 
walk around making racial statements all the time. 


More importantly, the State doesn’t have to prove a 
history of racism. Rather, the State just has to prove that 
[p. 32] the criminal acts that this defendant committed 
were done with the purpose to intimidate based on race. 
And I think the State clearly has met that burden. 


One other point raised by the defense. The defense 
called Dr. Cooke. Dr. Cooke testified that the defendant is 
easily led and just said what (sic) the police — just said to 
the police what they wanted to hear. Judge, he had four 
shootings here. The defendant only admitted to two of 
them to the police. So if Dr. Cooke’s testimony is to be 
believed, then this defendant would have admitted to all 
four of the shootings. 


Judge, I think the evidence overwhelmingly shows 
that the only possible motive for this crime was that he 
acted out of racial bias. 


First of all, in this case — most cases the State does not 
know the intent of the defendant. Most cases the State 
doesn’t know that. 


127a 


This case is different. The defendant told us why he ~ 
did what he did. He admitted to the police — he said, 
“Look, nothing personal against the Fowlkes. I don’t even 
know them. The reason I did this is because they’re black 
and I don’t want blacks in my neighborhood.” So this 
defendant admitted the reason why he committed this 
crime. 


The Fowlkes family is the only black family east of 
Lincoln Avenue in the City of Vineland. That's a two-mile 
[p. 33] stretch on Chestnut Avenue where both Mr. 
Apprendi and the Fowlkes resided. Mr. Apprendi said 
when he testified that he was only aware of one other 
family — one family, period, on East Chestnut Avenue that 
was black. He thought their name was the Lakes. He 
described the house as having a wagon wheel in front. 


And Mr. Fowlkes just testified that the family that 
resides there is a white family. 


More importantly, when Mr. Apprendi spoke with 
the police he admitted that he knew that black people 
lived at that residence that he fired at. And the reason he 
knew this was because he had frequently seen them out 
in the yard prior to the shooting. So that, therefore, he 
knew that a black family resided at that address. 


Mr. Fowlkes just corroborated that testimony in his 
testimony this morning, indicating that frequently he and 
his children would be outside the home. 


Mr. Apprendi claims this was not racially motivated. 
Judge, very importantly, this was not an isolated incident. 
This happened more than once. Was it just random 
chance happening over and over again that the Fowlkes 


128a 


family just happened to be fired at by Mr. Apprendi? I 
don’t think so, Judge. I think it’s clear that the reason was 
racially motivated. 


Finally, Judge, you have the black Santa Clause [sic] 
which [p. 34] was on the door. It’s pretty clear, Judge, that 
that was the target that Mr. Apprendi was shooting at on 
December 22nd. December 22nd is the count which is 
subject to the motion for extended term. Not only was he 
shooting at that black Santa Clause [sic], Judge, but Mr. 
Fowlkes testified that the bullets actually hit that black 
Santa Clause [sic]. 


Judge, frankly, there’s no other reasonable explana- 
tion why Charles Apprendi, who did not know the 
Fowlkes - the Fowlkes did not know him - why he would 
repeatedly fire at their home. There’s just no credible, 
rational reason, other than they were black. And, as Mr. 
Apprendi said, he wanted to send them a message that he 
did not want them in their (sic) neighborhood - in his 
neighborhood. Excuse me. 


THE COURT: Mr. O'Neill. 


MR. O’NEILL: If Your Honor pleases, if I may, 
I'd first like to treat the issue which first confronts the 
Court here today, that raised by the Court, that as to the 
standard of proof required of the prosecution before the 
Court could consider applying a racially motivated or 
bias to a crime so as to enhance the sentence. 


Now, I invite the Court's attention to the Supreme 
Court decision in State versus Biegenwald, which appears 
at 106 New Jersey 13, decided in 1987. And specifically at 
Page 59 of that opinion, the Supreme Court construed the 


129a 


statutory language pertaining to weighing aggravating 
and mitigating [p. 35] factors. And that’s what we’re 
doing here, Judge, as you know better than I, in sentenc- 
ing somebody. Before the Court can sentence someone, 
you have to consider - as, again, you know much better 
than I - the weight of the aggravating and mitigating 
factors. 


In dealing with that issue which is again before you 
today, the Supreme Court said in Biegenwald that the 
Supreme Court Approached the construction of the statu- 
tory language, quote, “With a long-standing practice in 
the criminal law of this State that the State has the burden 
to prove that element beyond a reasonable doubt.” 


Judge, if you look at Des Marets and acknowledge as 
Chief Justice Wilentz did at Page 69 that the statute itself, 
quote, “strongly suggests that the actor’s state of mind 
was meant to be irrelevant” - so the argument by the 
State that they don’t have to prove beyond a reasonable 
doubt that there was racial motivation is bogus. I think 
the Court has to be bound by the Supreme Court's deci- 
sion. The Supreme Court made that differentiation in 
stating very clearly that in Des Marets the state of mind of 
the defendant was irrelevant. 


In this particular case, the mens rea, the state of mind 
of the defendant, is not just relevant, it is essential so as 
to be evaluated by the Court. 


So, therefore, the statute is wrong. It flies in the face 
of the law. In fact, New Jersey Rules of Evidence, [p. 36] 
101(b)1, in defining what constitutes burdens of proof, it 


130a 


indicates on Comment 7 that, “In criminal case the stan- 
dard comprehended by this rule is that of, quote, ‘beyond 
a reasonable doubt.’ ” 


Judge, going back to long before the colonists wrote 
up the laws, long before the courts of yore decided cases 
in the State, coming down through the Blackstone tradi- 
tion, the burden of proof on the State is to prove anything 
involving the mind of a defendant. The reasons why he or 
she did something always must be proved beyond a 
reasonable doubt. 


And that’s what they have to prove here today. They 
haven't done it. 


Now, let’s talk about the facts of this case, Judge. 


The Prosecutor grandly brought out this rifle and 
pointed out to all of us that it has a laser beam on it. Now, 
if the defendant wanted to harm these people with a laser 
beam he certainly could have done so. He didn’t have to 
do so in the wee hours of the morning of (sic) any of 
those dates. He could have done so in the daylight where 
the laser beam could have directed his rounds, his car- 
tridges, into the bodies of any one of these poor victims. 
He didn’t do that. Doesn't that really suggest that there’s 
not a reason behind what he did to harm somebody? It’s 
something inexplicable as to normal people. This man is 
not a norma! person. We have uncontroverted testimony 
by Dr. Cooke, the professor at the [p. 37] University of 
Pennsylvania, that he has problems. He has problems 
forming judgments. He makes mistakes as to judgment. 
He does it because this is the way he thinks. He needs 
therapy. He needs treatment. Now, when you combine 


131la 


that preexisting condition of his mind with the synergis- 
tic or intertwining of facts of alcohol and drugs, then you 
have a worse situation for a man, as this man is, who has 
problems with judgment. 


Now, let’s talk about, for a few minutes, if the Court 
will indulge me, as to what happened on the morning of 
this December 22 of last year. We have a situation where 
Dr. Cooke has already testified and written in his report 
that this man, with this type of mind that has, is much 
more likely to be coerced, to be led into saying things that 
he does not believe. That remains uncontroverted. There 
was no report submitted by an expert for the State. There 
was no testimony produced by the State to be at odds 
with that opinion uttered by Dr. Cooke. 


The testimony was that this man, after imbibing in 
alcohol and drugs over many, many hours, did a very 
foolish thing, albeit a criminal thing, a wrong thing to do. 
But in sentencing this man the Court must look at all of 
the circumstances. In the words of the Court, the totality 
of the circumstances. It’s not enough to say, to hear, to 
read, that a house was damaged. It’s not enough to say a 
police officer testified that this man told him that he did 
this act or these [p. 38] acts because he didn’t want blacks 
in the neighborhood. That’s reprehensible to me and to 
any logical-thinking person in this day and age. It’s plain 
wrong. The reasons why it was uttered, the reasons why 
it was wrong, lie in the head of this man, which really has 
been examined at length by Dr. Cooke and not by any- 
body for the State. 


Dr. Cooke not only interviewed this man at length, he 
gave him four scientific tests. He had for his own review 


132a 


~ which he did, in fact, read and study — all the records of 
the Vineland Police Department, the indictment, medical 
notes from the jail, and so forth and so on. He had all of 
that discovery information. He testified that this man has 
evidence of being hypomanic. He testified that this man 
has had a drinking problem since he was age 19 when he 
was subjected to the pressures, the anxiety, of phar- 
maceutical school. He handled it the wrong way. He did a 
lot of crazy things. 


You heard testimony on September 5 from people 
who knew him. Among whom was his best friend, who 
submitted a report to you, who submitted testimony to all 
of us, of the goofy, wrong, silly, stupid, lacking-in-good- 
judgment things, that this defendant did. Not out of 
malice or hatred, but because he exercised poor judg- 
ment. 


The doctor rendered the opinion that there is a pat- 
tern of alcohol abuse. He also stated that he clearly has a 
pattern of drug dependence. This started due to the pres- 
sures [p. 39] of pharmacy school. They were made worse 
by his gastrointestinal problems to combat the pain. He, 
unfortunately, became drug dependent. When mixed with 
alcohol, as the doctor testified uncontrovertedly, it 
becomes worse. His poor judgment becomes even worse. 


The testing, the objective testing - not merely the 
interview or the clinical diagnosis, but the objective test- 
ing as testified to by the doctor and as appears on his 
(sic) - Page 6 of his report indicate (sic), quote, “Consid- 
erable pathology.” Meaning, as Your Honor knows, there 
is something wrong up there. Obsessive-compulsive traits 
are evident on the testing, as they were on the interview. 


133a 


He testified to that. Nobody said anything different. 
When he is disinhibited by drugs, alcohol, or emotional 
stimulation, then his condition becomes even worse. 


His diagnoses where obsessive-compulsive disorder, 
psychlosymic (phonetic) disorder, drug dependence, alco- 
hol abuse. 


He testified it was his opinion that at the time of the 
incident last reported upon, that the defendant suffered 
from these disorders. As a result of these disorders, his 
judgment and his impulse control were substantially 
impaired at the time. He certainly knew that he was 
discharging the weapon toward the house and that it was 
wrong. Therefore, he does not meet the criteria for insan- 
ity. However, the testing clearly [p. 40] shows that his 
anxiety can disrupt his attention, his concentration, his 
perception, his judgment, and his impulse control. These 
characteristics make him far more suggestible and far 
more easily coerced than the average individual. 


He testified also as he stated in his report on Page 8, 
that “I can offer the opinion that he is far more likely to 
admit to things that he did not do than would be the 
average individual. He has a strong need to remove him- 
self from emotionally-stimulating situations. And if he 
believed that telling the police things that would satisfy 
them would get him out of the situation, he would be 
quite likely to do so.” 


Now, if we take the report of the expert, uncon-

when questioned - when the defendant was questioned 
by Detective/Sergeant D’Augostine, we can never like - 
no thinking person can ever like what this man said as 


134a 


the reason why he did these things. But we can under-

Prosecutor said to him, “You lied to the Judge.” 


ip. 41] And he said, “Yes, I lied to the Judge.” 
“You lied to the Judge.” 


And he admitted it. Why? Because he’s that way. He 
will say things that are untrue to get himself out of — as

When Detective/Sergeant D’Augostine, who is a fine

Now, you know, you and I know - and I’m sure Mr.

137a 


This man is not. He’s not any of those things. And if 
we look at the report given by Dr. Grill (phonetic), [p. 44] 
indicating the life expectancy of the various drugs taken

goes on to talk about Dilaudid. Then he talks about 
Lomotil and the other drug the defendant took. 


Judge, we’re not dealing with a normal person. We’re 
dealing with a person who if he’s threatened, he wants to 
remove himself from a threatened atmosphere, will say 
almost anything, even if it’s not true, to get out of it. If he 
thought when Mr. Luther was cross-examining him and 
when you were asking him questions that if he gave you 
what he wanted — Mr. Luther wanted or what you wanted 
~ he would '< - got off that stand and removed from the 
situation quicker than he would do it because he can’t

You heard and you saw the testimony, the affidavits 
under oath, of the African-Americans who testified 
they’ve known this man for three years, going back to 
when he was a baby. Been in the family home on many 
occasions with him. Never saw any incident involving 
racial bias by this man. They bowled with him. He 
invited one of the black men over to his house for Super 
Bowl parties. They went out drinking and eating after 
bowling. They socialized regularly. He worked with these 
people. Never did any one of them see any evidence 
[p. 45] of racial bias. Here are people who put their

on the line to tell you what's right, so that you can make 
the right decision here, which is to find that, A, the law is 


138a 


invalid. There is no proof required by a preponderance. It

Two, that this man cannot be believed. What he said 
under stress to the detective, that he did it because he 
didn’t want blacks in the neighborhood, when the detec-

reason for the shootings was racial, as indicated by Ms. 
Fowlkes. 


So, therefore, Judge, in conclusion as to these issues,

THE COURT: Very well. Gentlemen, as you're 
well aware, the statute which we’re talking about, 
2C:44-3, provides for an enhanced penalty when the 
Court is satisfied by a preponderance of the evidence that 
the actions of a defendant are motivated by racial bias. 
Now, the statute does not make speaking eligible for 
enhanced penalty. What it makes eligible for an enhanced 
penalty is some action on the part of the [p. 46] defendant 
that causes harm to someone else or some other persons 


or property. 


And I think the Court can quickly dispose of the 
argument that the statute’s unconstitutional. I’m satisfied 
that this case of State versus Des Marets, 92 New Jersey, 
Page 62, a 1983 case, which was authorized by Chief 
Justice Wilentz, is not applicable to that argument. 


139a 


The Court in that case said that the actor’s state of 
mind was meant to be irrelevant. It was then talking 
about the Graves Act. With regard to elements of a crime, 
there is no question about the fact that those elements 
have to be proved to the satisfaction to the trier of fact by 
proof beyond a reasonable doubt. 


But the issue here is one which the Legislature has 
left to the discretion of the Court. And if the Court is 
satisfied by a preponderance of the evidence that there is 
racial motivation in the actions of a defendant, then the 
Enhanced Penalty Statute is applicable. 


My notes indicate that both parties agreed that the 
Miranda warnings were given and that the statement 
given to the police was voluntarily and knowingly given. 


Tie defendant contends that portions of the state- 
ment, wherein he admits that the shootings resulted from 
racial bias, were not true. He maintains that the sugges- 
tion of racial bias first came from the interviewing officer. 
And [p. 47] that, furthermore, he did not know until 
being questioned at the police station that the house at 
which he fired was the residence of African-Americans. 


Now, to support that contention, the statement he 
gave to the police was not true, there was introduced to 
the Court testimony of a Dr. Gerald Cooke, a clinical and 
forensic psychologist. The doctor apparently interviewed 
the defendant while he was incarcerated in the County 
Jail. And his ultimate opinion was supported by the 
results of that interview, the doctor’s review of police 
reports, and the administering to the defendant of a 
series of personality tests. 


140a 


The doctor found that the defendant was heavily into 
drugs and alcohol. Found him to be, among other things, 
anxious, insecure, with feelings of poor self-image and 
inadequacy. The doctor, furthermore, found the defen- 
dant to be obsessive and compulsive. And I’m reading 
from his report, “And one who projects his problems onto 
others. That he becomes very angry when under the 
influence of alcohol.” 


And here the defendant says that he was under the 
influence of alcohol, having had a number of drinks prior 
- shortly prior to the incident. 


Now, the doctor’s opinion is supportive of the defen- 
dant’s condition that he lied to the police merely to 
extricate himself from an uncomfortable situation. In 
other words, to stop the interrogation. 


[p. 48] Dr. Cooke candidly admitted, however, that he 
could not tell if Mr. Apprendi was, in fact, now telling the 
truth about his motive for firing at the house. The most 
that the doctor said that he could opine was that the 
defendant's lying to get out of an unpleasant situation 
would be consistent with his test results. In other words, 
consistent with personality traits revealed by the tests. 


The testimony of the defendant before this Court 
earlier this month is that the first suggestion that he 
might have acted out of bias came from the interrogating 
officer. And that to quickly end the interrogation, which 
he apparently found seriously unpleasant, he adopted the 
police officer’s suggestion by admitting that he fired at 
the house because it was occupied by Afro-Americans. 
He buttressed his testimony that he - that the police 
made the suggestion by saying that the police described 


l4la 


to him the horrors of jail. And, thus, making him even 


more pliable. He denies ever having any bias toward 
blacks. 


The officer, Dennis D’Augostine, denied that the 
police planted this bias motive. The police report itself 
reflects that the defendant admitted knowing black per- 
sons lived at the house because he had seen them outside. 
That’s what he told the police on the night of the inter- 
rogation. An that he was giving them a message by firing 
at the house. 


Now, the testimony of the officer is corroborative of 
[p. 49] the report. And, furthermore, the defendant, 
according to the report (sic) - according to the testimony 
of the officer, did not appear intoxicated. He answered 
the questions clearly, according to the testimony of Detec- 
tive D’Augostine. And, furthermore, the detective’s testi- 
mony — I think the report reflects that questioning ceased 
when the defendant requested counsel. 


Now, the Court heard testimony and received letters 
from acquaintances, friends and relatives, all to the effect 
that the defendant was not a racist and that he never 
expressed racial remarks. And that he is not racially 
biased. Some of these letters and reports came from black 
persons. An affidavit - in the form of affidavits and 
letters. 


And I might say in passing that I think everybody 
here recognizes the fact that persons who have racial bias 
don’t wear that bias as some badge of honor. I mean, the 
remarks that people make of that nature — those remarks 
are protected by the First Amendment - usually are done 
behind closed doors. And people just don’t take action on 


142a 


those (sic) bias. So that these people who submit these 
letters don’t know, obviously, what has gone on in the 
mind of the defendant. 


Now, at the entry of his plea of guilty before this 
Court on July the 24th, the defendant under oath said 
that the purpose of his firing the gun was to scare some- 
one. 


And I refer you gentlemen to the transcript. I [p. 50] 
ordered a copy of it. I think I sent you copies of it. Page 
13, Line (sic) 1 through 4, and Page 28, Lines 19 through 
25. 


Now, since then the defendant has written to the 
Court three or four letters since the entry of his plea. And 
all those letters were shared with both the prosecution 
and defense counsel. And those letters, at least in part, 
attempt to negate what the defendant purportedly told 
the police and what he told the Court under oath when 
he entered his plea. 


And his newly-adopted position is that he never 
intended to scare anyore when he fired the shots. That he 
was not clear on (sic) July - in July when he — because he 
was “overwhelmed with lawyers and detectives” — and I 
quote from the letter - “and prosecutors, and he wasn't 
thinking clearly.” His present position, or at least his 
position in the letters, is - as | understand it, is that he 
fired the shots only to break something. 


And I might say that we didn’t have before us - or I 
didn’t know - that the door of the Fowlkes residence was 
not purple, as described by the defendant. It was almost 
totally clear glass, according to Mr. Fowlkes this morning, 


143a 


with a 3-inch burgundy border. So I, quite frankly, have 
difficulty in believing that the defendant was attracted to 
this door by the color. And that something went off in his 
mind under this (sic) — in his intoxicated state of mind 
that he wanted to destroy the door. 


[p. 51] Now, the Court finds the testimony of Detec- 
tive D’Augostine to be credible, believable. And there’s 
no reason to believe that the police initiated the idea of 
the reason for firing the gun. 


Both sides, in stipulating there was no Miranda issue, 
impliedly conceded that the questioning by the police 
was not overbearing. It was not onerous or is not in any 
sense dictatorial. That it was relatively brief. And evi- 
dence that it was not burdensome can be taken from the 
fact that the questioning stopped when the defendant 
requested a lawyer. One need look no further than the 
defendant’s own statement to the police and to the Court 
to be satisfied that the crime was motivated by racial bias. 
The defendant contends that he did not know that blacks 
resided in the home. 


And the question has to be asked, as I think Mr. 
Luther asked it this morning: Why then did the defen- 
dant pick this particular target out not once, but at least 
twice as his target? No other Afro-Americans lived in the 
neighborhood. His excuse that he became incensed by the 
door and its color is bizarre and not accepted by the 
Court, particularly in !ight of Mr. Fowlkes testimony this 
morning. 


The Court's satisfied that at the entry of his plea he 
once again admitted his motive. And is trying by his 


144a 


letters to explain away his statement so as to avoid the 
punishment for a biased crime. 


[p. 52] I went to the trouble, quite frankly, gentlemen, 
of ordering the tape of the plea. Those proceedings lasted 
approximately 30 minutes. And there was nothing in that 
proceeding that was so overbearing as to cause the defen- 
dant to misstate his plea, which he is attempting to do 
through the use of these letters. 


The defendant has presented himself as articulate. 
He’s intelligent and in full control of his faculties. He’s 
educated. And the Court is satisfied that the defendant 
said and meant what he said when questioned by the 
police. And I’m satisfied that when the Court took the 
plea that he said what he meant and meant what he said. 


Accordingly, the Court finds the standard of proof 
required by the statute has been met. The defendant is 
subject to enhanced penalties. 


Now, that’s not to say that the defendant’s without 
psychiatric problems. I think any fair reading of Dr. 
Fowlkes (sic) - of Dr. Cooke’s report would indicate that 
he’s got serious problems that may have been [ex]acer- 
bated by the use of alcohol and drugs. But the use of 
alcohol and drugs and the treatment of his psychiatric 
problem is a medical problem. The Court can’t treat med- 
ical problems. All I can do is attempt to treat the social 
problems. 


And let’s not forget that the intoxication allegedly 
that the defendant was under is not alleged in this case 


by way [p. 53] of negating purposefulness or knowledge. 
That would have been a defense if this case had been 


145a 


tried, but it wasn’t urged as a defense. The defendant in 
this case has pled guilty. 


The Court's satisfied that it was an appropriate plea. 
And in fashioning the sentence I'll take into consideration 
the psychiatric problems. 


The State has an interest in protecting its citizens 
from crimes of this nature. And the Court has dictated an 
extended term. It’s not discretionary, it’s mandatory once 
that threshold question’s been answered. And I intend to 
impose the enhanced penalty. 


Now, I'll hear you on sentencing. 
Let me tell you something. 
MR. O’NEILL: May it please the Court,

149a 


These prevented him from contemplating that his con-

house. 


Now, he testified, the defendant did. He told the

black people in the premises, but really by this door. But, 
Judge, in - I think the important, the salient part, of this 
Mitigating Factor Number 2 would be that putting every- 
thing together, the totality of the circumstances, if you 
will, that it’s uncontroverted that this man suffers from 
substantially impaired judgment and impulse control. 
And these are the reasons why he does the things that we 
think are so wrong - are so wrong. That’s why he does 
them. And I think the Court should take that under 
consideration in passing sentence. 


The next mitigating circumstance is Number 4. The 
doctor made it clear that there is not insanity here. But [p. 
58] because of this preexisting problem that this defen- 
dant has, he suffers from self-defeating psychological

150a 


my virtually the same questions. And that’s significant. It

Mitigating Circumstance Number 7, Judge, the defen- 
dant has no prior history of delinquency or criminal 
activity. He’s never been convicted of a crime. 


Number 10, the Pre-Sentence Investigation reports on 
Page 8 that the defendant would respond favorably to

Number 11, this is a problem in the sense that not

the longer he’s away the more likely it is that they’re 
going to get worse. Your Honor has already decided to 
sentence him under the racially-enhanced statute. There-

aware of this, he might be subjected to some danger. 


Number 12, it’s indicated by the Pre-Sentence repor-

ment authorities. 


Judge, this is a situation, as I indicated earlier, in 
which there is no question that jail is necessary. Society 
has to understand that people who commit crimes must 
be punished for them. In this particular instance jail is 
appropriate. But I suggest to the Court that you might 
well consider the length of the sentence in terms of the 
totality of the circumstances that have been brought 
before you. 


Most significantly, that testified to by the doctor, 
uncontroverted as to the reasons why this man has done 
these bad things. Lack of good judgment, lack of impulse 
control, the synergistic effect of the drugs, the alcohol, the 


15la 


lasting effect of the drugs, as indicated by Dr. Grill in his

gest that the term imposed by the Court should not be the 
maximum term. It should be a term in keeping with the 
circumstances here. A term in keeping with his mental 
condition and his propensity for rehabilitation, as indi- 
cated in the Pre-Sentence Report.

Just briefly, Mr. O’Neill indicates Mitigating Factor 
Number 2 is present here, that the defendant did not 
contemplate that his conduct would cause or threaten 


serious harm. Judge, I think clearly that that’s not appli-

52a 


September 24, the bullet that was fired entered a 
bedroom window where 8-year-old Michael Fowlkes was 
sleeping. It was later recovered in the hallway of the 
home. And December 22, there were numerous bullets, a 
veritable barrage of bullets, that were fired at the house, 
most of which, again, entered into the premises of the 
home. One of the bullets actually lodged into Dawn 
Fowlkes bedroom wall. So to say that he didn’t contem- 
plate that his conduct would cause or threaten serious 
harm is just not accurate. 


[p. 61] And that doesn’t even get into this psycho- 
logical damage that the Fowlkes have suffered as a result 
of these attacks. 


Mr. O'Neill also indicates that the defendant would 
respond affirmatively to probationary treatment. Judge, I 
don’t think that that factor should be found here. First of 
all is the repetitive nature of this crime. This was not an 
isolated incident. This happened on more than one occa- 
sion. And, also, Dr. Cooke, the defense’s own psycholo- 
gist, indicate that the defendant is unpredictable and 
should be watched carefully. 


So that’s all that the State has to add at this point

choices that an individual has made towards us. 
Unprovoked, I might add.

becoming a pharmacist. And to waste his time, effort, to

home is unacceptable, Your Honor, in our [f. 63] county.

for years, Your Honor. We had the Williams s.ooting with

cross burnings, Your Honor.

very ~ | realize that it is very wrong. And I am very SOrry. 
I am honestly very sorry that I did this. I know I deserve 
to be punished for - for what I did. 


But as far as the racial issue, there are so many lies 
and distortions and twisting of facts in this case that it 
would take me at least three hours to explain it to the 
Court. And I - I've tried to do that before. It’s just not - 
it’s just not true. My statement was not voluntary. It was 
~ | was - I was just pounded by that detective. And then 
he goes on the stand and lies about it. I can’t do anything 
about that. 


As far as this black Santa on the door, I never - this - 
right now is the first time in the past nine months that 
I've ever heard of this fact about this black Santa. I didn’t 
see any black Santa on the door. 


And the door - whether I call it purple or burgundy 
has — it’s ridiculous. It shouldn’t even have been men- 
tioned It attracted my eye. There was —- the door - there 
was more than - not more than a 6-inch - there was more 
than a 6-inch [p. 66] border of purple or burgundy. The 
bottom of the - of the door was solid from what I can 
remember. 


I never meant to hurt or scare anybody. I thank God 
that nobody was hurt. I have never tried to hurt anybody 
in my whole life. And my 40 years of life is my proof that 


157a 


racism is not a part of my life. I’ve interacted with - with 
black people, Puerto Rican people. You know, anything, 
everybody. I have never had a problem. 


The whole thing is - is just absurd. I - I was mislead 
by - by extreme pressure from the - from the detective. 


And as far as admitting to scaring - to — to wanting 
to scare somebody when I took that plea agreement, | 
wouldn’t answer that question that Mr. Luther presented 
to me because it was - it was not true. And I was under 
the impression that it was - how it was reworded by Mr. 
O'Neill, that I was not admitting to scaring anybody. 


Well, you know, of course, people are going to think 
of the testimony. And they’re going to say, “What was I 
thinking of? Why did I do it?” I’ve been asking myself 
this question for the past nine months, Your Honor. I 
wasn't thinking, period. It’s not what I was thinking. I 
wasn't thinking. I wasn’t rational. I was under the influ- 
ence of alcohol and drugs. 


And as far as the drugs being out of my system, 
which [p. 67] Mr. Luther suggested, it’s just not true. one 
of the drugs, Ativan, has a half-life of 20 hours. I took 
that about 1:30 ii the morning or something like that. 


I didn’t - another thing is I didn’t get up in the 
middle of the night and decide to go shoot at a house. I 
was at ~ I was at the bar. I was at the bar until like 1:30. 
And then the detective says I was there ‘till 12 o'clock. 
That's not truth (sic) - it’s not the truth. I didn’t go home 
and go to sleep. I was intoxicated. 


There’s no other reason ever for anybody to ever, you 
know ~ I mean, like - like I’m agreeing with Mr. Fowlkes. 


158a 


In this day of (sic) age somebody should be - somebody 
should be damned and condemned if — if the truth is that 
it was done for racial purposes. But this is not the truth, 
Your Honor. 


Another thing about asking for counsel to stop the 
testimony. I never asked for counsel. I never had a law- 
yer. I didn’t have — I didn’t want to call my lawyer. At the 
time when I admitted these things, I had gone about 23 
hours with no sleep. And the - the additive effects of the 
central nervous system depressant with alcohol —- I was so 
- I was so exhausted. That’s why I stopped the testimony. 
I didn’t know what I was doing. I was - I was in a fog. 


But the most important thing is that — that race was - 
had nothing to do with this case, Your Honor. None at all. 
And I am very — I am very sorry for what I did. 


[p. 68] Tnat’s the — the most important things that I 
have to say. 


I hope the Fowlkes maybe someday can understand. I 
realize that they’re not going to forget me right now, but I 
just hope that someday maybe they can. 


THE COURT: All right. Let me say this to you, 
Mr. Apprendi, and to Mr. Fowlkes. 


The Court does not — the maximum sentence in 
this case is 20 years. And that’s because it’s an enhanced 
penalty that normally would have been ten years. And 
you're being exposed to that range of punishment as the 
punishment that you receive for a racially-biased crime. 


And I might say that I don’t have to look - I could 
forget about the police report. And from all the other 
evidence before me, the Court made a - could have made 


159a 


a decision that’s supported by all the other evidence that 
what you did was racially motivated. 


If your position is that your mind was so clouded by 
alcohol and drugs that you didn’t know what you were 
doing, then you should have tried your case and used 
that as a defense that you didn’t have the requisite 
knowledge or purposefulness that would have satisfied 
the statute. 


Particularly one in your situation where you have a - 
where you have training and education and the use of 
drugs. I find that to be unacceptable. The Court's not 
going to [p. 69] excuse one wrong, the firing at the house, 
by using another wrong, which is the overuse of drugs 
and alcohol if that, in fact, did take place. 


On Indictment 00046, the 18th count — 


Before I do that I might well take into consideration 
the aggravating and mitigating factors that have been 
urged by the Prosecutor and defense counsel. 


With regard to the mitigating factors, let me say, first 
of all, that Number 4, that there were substantial grounds 
tending to excuse or justify the conduct of the defendant, 
is rejected as a applicable mitigating factor. 


Likewise, Number 2, that the defendant did not con- 
template that his conduct would cause or threaten serious 
harm, is likewise rejected. Whether he intended to hurt 
somebody, whether he intended to scare someone, or 
whether he intended to do property damage, it’s inesca- 
pable that his conduct was designed to cause some type 
of harm. It probably caused almost the worse type of 
harm, which is psychological harm, to the Fowlkes. 


160a 


The Court finds that Number 6, that he will compen- 
sate the victims for their financial loss, is a consideration. 
I'll accept the note that I received from the probation 
officer dated September 22nd, that she - and I read “The 
victim phoned and reported an uncompensated loss of 
$1,900 property damage and $80 in medical bills for coun- 
seling because [p. 70] of the incident.” 


And the Court will order restitution in that amount. 
If there’s any argument with it by the Prosecutor or 
defense counsel, make an application within the next 30 
days and I'll give you a restitution hearing. 


The Court determines that Number 7 - partic- 
ularly applicable in this case. The defendant has no his- 
tory of prior delinquency or criminal activity and has led, 
prior to this, a law-abiding life. 


Likewise, Number 8, the Court finds to be applicable 
that his conduct was the result of circumstances unlikely 
to recur, particularly in view of the fact that he’s 
acknowledged his culpability. And he’s obviously headed 
for incarceration. 


The Court finds Number 2 and Number 9 of the 
aggravating factors applicable in this case. 


And the Court also takes into consideration that, as I 
said before, I’m satisfied the defendant has a psychiatric 
problem. And I’m satisfied, quite frankly, that this racial 
bias would not have manifested itself, except for the 
psychiatric problem that he has. 


Presumptive sentence at the enhanced level is 15 
years. The obligation of the Court is to weigh the things 
that I’ve mentioned. 


léla 


And, accordingly, on the 18th count, the sentence of 
the Court is that you be committed to the custody of the 
[p. 71] Commissioner of Corrections for a period of 12 
years. Under the Graves Act there’s a mandatory penalty. 
And the Court imposes a four-year period of parole inel- 
igibility. 

The sentence on the third count is that you be com- 
mitted to the custody of the Commissioner of Corrections 
for a period of seven years, with a period of parole 
ineligibility of three years. 


Now, the Court’s aware that all of these were inde- 
pendent acts. But, nevertheless, I’m satisfied that they 
represent an aberration. And, accordingly, the counts will 
all run concurrent to each other. 


On the 22nd count the sentence of the Court is that 
you be sentenced to three years in the New Jersey State 
Prison And, as I said, this sentence is to run concurrent to 
the sentence on the other three (sic) counts - on the other 
two counts. 


There’s a hundred-dollar Violent Crime Commission 


penalty and a $75 Safe Street Act penalty on each of the 
counts. 


The collection of the fines and penalties, as well as 
the restitution, is transferred to the prison authorities. 


And if there’s difficulty, Mr. O’Neill, with the type of 
crime and his safety in the prison, that’s something that 
he’ll have to wrestle with. And that’s up to the — that’s a 
problem of the corrections facility. I can’t help that. 


[p. 72] The balance of the counts, as I understand it, 
Prosecutor, are to be dismissed? 


162a 


MR. LUTHER: Yes, Your Honor. 
THE COURT: All right. The defendant - 
So ordered. 


- the defendant has 45 days within which to appeal 
this sentence. If he wishes to do and cannot afford an 
attorney, one will be appointed to represent him. Any- 
thing else to come before the Court on this. matter? 


MR. LUTHER: No, Your Honor.

MR. O'NEILL: Judge, one other thing. D-1 
should be admitted into evidence. Introduce that. That's 
the -
