GERALD COOKE, M.D., WITNESS, SWORN 
THE COURT: Your full name is? 
THE WITNESS: Gerald Cooke - C-O-O-K-E. 


205a 


DIRECT EXAMINATION BY MR. O’NEILL: 


Q. Doctor, at my -equest did you have occasion to 
examine the defendant in the Cumberland County Jail 
back on February 15 of 1995? 


G. Cooke - Direct 
[p. 40] A. Yes. 


Q. Now, Doctor, what was the purpose of the eval- 
uation as you understood it to be? 


A. Well, the primary purpose initially is to under- 
stand the psychological functioning and then apply that 
to an understanding of his reasons for committing the 
offense. And also to address issues of whether he is the 
kind of individual who could be easily coerced or influ- 
enced during an interrogation to make statements that 
were not accurate statements. 


Q. Doctor, prior to your examination of the defen- 
dant and your taking a history and formulating a clinical 
opinion, did I provide to you various records, such as the 
Vineland Police Department reports and the indictment 
and medical notes from the Cumberland County Jail, and 
also a ballistics reports from the State Police? Did you 
examine all of those? 


A. Yes, you did. And I did review those. 


Q. All right. Doctor, did you have occasion to 
administer any objecting testing to this defendant? 


A. Yes. 


206a 


Q. What tests did you administer? And explain 
briefly, Doctor, what each of them is. 


A. Okay. The first, Your Honor, was the Minnesota 
Multiphasic Personality Inventory 2, known a. the 
MMPI2. This is the most widely used objective person- 
ality test. It’s [p. 41] particularly valuable in forensic 
cases because it has three validity scales that tell us 
whether the individual is approaching the testing in a 
candid manner or exaggerating problems that aren’t there 
or denying problems that are there. So it’s valuable for 
that. 


It then has a series of clinical scales that provides 
information on the individual’s symptoms, problems, 
personality dynamics, things of that nature. 


I also gave him an incomplete sentences blank. That’s 
a projective personality test. And that is used to get 
information about conscious attitudes and values, the 
perception and quality of interpersonal relationships, 
perceptions of himself, things of that nature. 


I gave him the raw shock inkblot technique. That is a 
series of inkblots. It’s also a projective personality test. 
It’s designed to push a little deeper into what some 
people might call the unconscious. It also reveals under- 
lying conflicts, fears, wishes. And also tells us how accu- 
rately the individual perceives reality. And if there are 
distortions or interferences with perception, what some 
of the psychological reasons for that might be. 


I then also gave him three sub-tests of the Wechsler 
Adult Intelligence Scale, Revised. And the purpose of 
that was not only to get a rough estimate of intellectual 


207a 


functioning — I didn’t give the whole test, so what I have 
is just an estimate. [p. 42] But also to see if there’s any 
interference with intellectual functioning due to emo- 
tional or psychological factors. 


Q. Doctor, upon arriving at the interview with the 
defendant, how did he appear to you? 


A. Well, both during the interview and during my 
testing I found him to be anxious. He had what I might 
call a good deal of nervous energy. But it also went to the 
extent in terms of his speech. The speed of his speech, his 
affect or emotional tone, I would describe as hypomanic. 
And that is that — fast, somewhat pressured kind of 
speech that’s often associated with an affective or emo- 
tional tone disorder. 


Part of the result of that was that his attention and 
concentration was not good. He listened very poorly to 
my questions. And I had to repeat my test instructions or 
get him to stop and listen. I had to specifically focus him 
so he would listen to instructions, rather than jumping 
right into things. 


He was self-critical about his responses and perfor- 
mance to the tests. And I also found in giving - in doing 
the interview portion that he had what I would call 
obsessive-compulsive traits. And I'll get into that more 
because those also appeared on both the objective and 
projective personality testing. 


I did not find that he was psychotic. I did not find 
that he had any schizophrenia or thinking disorder or 
anything of [p. 43] that nature. 


208a 


Q. So this defendant is not insane and cannot 
invoke any kind of an insanity defense or a mitigating 
circumstances defense; is that your finding? 


A. In my opinion that is correct. 


Q. All right. Now, Doc, you took a history as you 
always do; didn’t you? 


A. That’s correct. 


Q. And you found as part of that history that appar- 
ently his mother is hypomanic and has gastrointestinal 
problems like he does; is that right? 


A. Based on his description — he didn’t use the term 
“hypomanic,” but based on his description - of course, I 
haven't seen her, but it appeared so from his description. 


Q. All right. Then you went through his education, 
the fact he became a pharmacist. And you went through 
his social history, his dating relationships; did you not? 


A. That’s correct. 


Q. All right. Now, did you have occasion to explore 
his contact with alcohol and/or drugs? 


A. Yes. 

Q. And what did you find as to that, Doctor? 
A. Well, - 

Q. Was there any pattern that you found? 

A. Oh, absolutely. 

[p. 44] Q. What was that, sir? 


209a 


A. Well, I always start out by asking how - when 
they started using alcohol or drugs. He started drinking 
at age 19. He usually drinks beer, but on weekends he 
says he would drink vodka and tonics. He told me that he 
has a low tolerance to alcohol and gets high very fast. 
And that is very consistent with the testing. There are 
certain types of individuals with certain personality char- 
acteristics that seem to be more susceptible to alcohol. 
And he has that. 


The pattern he described to me was a pattern of 
alcohol abuse. I couldn’t carry it to the next level of 
saying alcohol dependency, though I suspect that it may 
be. Part of the reason for that was that he is also simul- 
taneously using drugs. And it’s hard for him to evaluate 
how much of what he experiences is due to alcohol and 
how much is due to drugs. 


He told me he started using drugs at age 21. Primar- 
ily opiates and most frequently Lomotil for both pain and 
depression. He tells me he’s used it daily since age 21 to 
varying amounts. Sometimes a little, but sometimes quite 
a bit. He’s also used anticolon (sic) - anticholinergics. 
And he says - and he’s a pharmacist, I’m not - but he 
says they have a synergistic effect that one increases the 
effects of others. I can’t comment on that. That's just his 
indication. 


Q. Did you find there was any pattern of drug 
dependence, Doctor? 


[p. 45] A. Absolutely. When you use those kinds of 
drugs to those (sic) degree daily for - at the time I saw 
him like 19 years or 18 years, that is clearly a pattern of 
drug dependence. 


210a 


Q. Doctor, did you go into the events leading up to 
the charges for which he has pleaded guilty? 


A. Yes, I did. 


Q. All right; what did you find in that respect, 
Doctor? 


A. Well, he indicated to me that he had lived in his 
house for about six years. He did not know when the 
Fowlkes family moved in. He told me that he did not 
know that the family that lived in that house was black 
until he was told that by the detective during the inter- 
rogation. 


He also says that the detective told him about prior 
incidents. And he told me he didn’t remember commit- 
ting those acts. What he tells me 


Q. That was the month before, I take it, November 
of ‘94? 


A. That’s correct. 


As part of taking his history of alcohol and drug use, 
he told me that he gets violent thoughts whenever he 
drinks to excess. And he told me that that’s what hap- 
pened on 12/22/94, as well, that he says he had feelings 
that he wanted to destroy something. He had feelings 
that he wanted to do something destructive, to do - and 
he has had such feelings before and has acted on them 
before. He told me about a prior incident of [p. 46] 
getting drunk and ripping a gate off a house. Things of 
that nature. 


Excuse me. And he describes what he did as, quote, 
“An impulsive, destructive thing.” He says that the door 


21la 


caught his attention because it was purple and had a 
large pane of glass. And he just had the thought to 
destroy it. And that’s why he shot at it. He just had a 
feeling on the way home from the bar while he was drunk 
and drug-intoxicated that he wanted to break something. 
And he told me it didn’t matter what it was. And that 
caught his attention. 


Now, I went back with him over the course of his 
alcohol and drug use during that day. And he indicated 
to me that it started about 12:30 in the afternoon. And 
though there were times - there were some times during 
the day that he drank, sometimes he used drugs and 
sometimes he did neither. But that overall in the period 
from 12:30 in the afternoon to around 1 a.m. he believes 
he consumed approximately eight vodka and tonic. 


There were two different periods when he drinks. 
And he said I - about the first period that he thought he 
had four, more or less. And the second time three or four. 
So it could be six, it could be nine. The best estimate is 
about eight from what he told me. 


He also took four to six Lomotil, one Dilaudid and 
two Ativan. 


[p. 47] And he told me that after the shooting he 
went home, put the gun behind the door of the attic, and 
went to sleep. And told me that he was in bed about 20 
minutes when the police arrived. He told me that during 
the time he spoke to the police — well, first at the house 
and later at the station, that he still felt intoxicated from 
the drugs and alcohol during that time. 


212a 


He also told me that at first - you know, he acknowl- 
edged ~ I had the records — that at first he told the police 
he didn’t do it. Then he thought if he cooperated with 
them — his term would be they, quote, “They would be 
less stringent” unquote, with him. He told me that, for 
example, he did not know it was a black family. Yet when 
they told him that he indicated that that’s the reason he 
gave (sic) the shooting - he did the shooting. 


He says that he was embarrassed. He says that they 
pressured him. He was scared. He was anxious. These are 
the things he told me, but they’re certainly highly consis- 
tent with my analysis of his personality. And he said the 
officer basically said to him, “If you make it easier for us, 
we'll make it easier for you.” And so he went overboard, 
according to what he told me, trying to cooperate with 
them just to get out of the situation as quickly as possible. 


Q. Now, Doctor, can you - do you have an opinion - 
and by the way, each of the opinions that you rendered in 
this case [p. 48] must be within a reasonable degree of 
psychological probability to be admissible in court. I 
want you to understand that. And have your opinions 
expressed so far been consistent with that standard? 


A. Yes, and - 

Q. And if you can’t answer any of my questions or 
the Prosecutor’s questions within that standard, within a 
reasonable degree of psychological probability, you will 
so tell us or tell the Court; will you? 


A. I can, yes. And those apply to my psychological 
testing, findings and my opinions about the - what I 
sometimes refer to as the psycho-legal questions. 


213a 


Q. Doctor, can you tell us within that standard 
whether the psychological objective testing that you 
administered was consistent with the information that 
you obtained from the interview and the history? 


A. Yes, it was, absolutely. 


Q. And was it also consistent with your clinical 
impression that you formulated upon meeting him and 
seeing him during the period of time you were in the jail? 


A. Yes. 


Q. All right. Now, did that objective testing indicate 
whether or not there is pathology as to this defendant? 


A. Yes. 
Q. Okay; what did it indicate? 


[p.49] A. Okay. First of all, he is an anxious individ- 
ual. And his anxiety interferes with his attention and his 
concentration. He has an extremely poor self-image, par- 
ticularly with a person who has his level of academic and 
occupational accomplishments. The measures we have of 
identity and self-esteem and self-concept - we find that if 
a person has achieved academically and occupationally 
that they generally have a stronger self-image. And his is 
very weak, despite that. Along with anxiety. He is very 
insecure. He’s a very sensitive person. He feels very 
inadequate and insecure as a man. And part of what 
happens, I think, with these violent thoughts is a way of 
compensating for those feelings of inadequacy. 


I also mention that on interview he showed some 
obsessive-compulsive traits. And that was evident on the 
testing, as well. What that means is he tends to have a 


214a 


very rigid personality structure. He’s very concrete. He’s 
very literal. He also is literally obsessed with collecting 
things and accumulating things. Part of obsessive-com- 
pulsiveness is also perfectionism or a desire for perfec- 
tionism. And I think what happens is that he sets certain 
goals for himself that are beyond his reach, so they’re not 
possible for him. And then when he cannot reach them he 
ends up having sort of chronic ongoing feelings of dissat- 
isfaction and anger. 


He also tends to project his problems. So rather than, 
for example, seeing his own contribution through rela- 
tionship [p. 50] problems with women, for example, he 
tends to project the problems onto others. Saying, for 
example, “Well, it’s the fault of other men. They interfere. 
They’re jealous of me.” Things of that nature. 


He does not have a very positive view of people. He 
generally anticipates that people will be annoying, dis- 
honest, things of that nature. I think he has never felt that 
he fits in with people. He says that he was very shy and 
bashful as a child. And the testing shows that he’s very 
fearful of rejection from other people, particularly 
women. 


He has a high tension level. He’s got a low frustra- 
tion level. A low level of - low tolerance from both 
frustration and anger. Little things that wouldn’t bother 
most people, as a result, annoy him. And in taking his 
occupational history, for example, he did indicate to me 
dealing with people on a day-to-day basis — the things he 
complained about were the things that we all deal with 
on a day-to-day basis; people being impatient, people 
wanting to get waited on quickly. Things of that nature. 


215a 


Yet, because of his chronic tension and because of his 
obsessiveness, he had a great deal of difficulty dealing 
with that. 


The testing also shows that his anxiety has what we 
call a somatic component. That is - 


Q. What do you mean by that, Doctor? 
A. — that is, it comes out in physical symptoms. 
[p. 51] Gastrointestinal symptoms, as well. 


Q. Is that the reason he was taking the drugs you 
indicated earlier, the Lomotil and the Dilaudid, and so 
forth? 


A. Well, that’s the reason he gives that he was tak- 
ing the drugs. In part, for the pain and the physical 
symptoms. In fact, though I don’t think he has insight 
into it completely, the drugs also helped him blot out a lot 
of these kinds of feelings I’m talking about, so that he 
didn’t have to live with them day to day. And so that that 
high tension level that he constantly felt could be reduced 
by the drugs. 


The testing also suggested that he would go through 
cycles. Part of that will be that at times he'll have this sort 
of hypomanic nervous energy and do a lot that I men- 
tioned. And other times he’ll be depressed and lethargic. 
I also see a cycle in terms of having these sorts of angry 
kinds of thoughts and then drinking and doing some- 
thing and then feeling genuine remorse and guilt after- 
wards. But not having the coping mechanisms to deal 
with what caused it in the first place. So even though he 
is remorseful and doesn’t want to do it again, the tension 
builds up again and the cycle repeats itself. 


216a 


I already mentioned he has to be concrete, very literal 
and very precise. And that need within him leads to 
distortions in perception. There are also distortions in 
perception due to his concerns about masculinity, his 
feeling inadequate as a man. And when these kinds of 
feelings arise he [p. 52] shows interference with not only 
the control of his feelings and his thoughts and his behav- 
ior, but also with his ability to perceive things accurately, 
to concentrate. Things of that nature. 


Q. Doctor, let me ask you this: What, if anything, 
does the objective testing suggest in terms of anger or 
handling anger? 


A. He exists at two levels. His personality is not 
well integrated. On the outside he has this obsessive- 
compulsive sort of thing which leads to overcontrol of the 
anger. But underneath the testing shows there’s a great 
deal of anger. And what happens is that when he over- 
does the drugs or the alcohol, particularly alcohol, or is in 
an emotionally-arousing situation, then we call the pro- 
cess disinhibition. The angry impulses that are usually 
inhibited and overcontrolled are disinhibited. That's 
when they emerge. And that’s when he does the kinds of 
things that led to these charges. 


Q. Doctor, did you come to a diagnosis of this indi- 
vidual? 


A. I came to six diagnoses of him, yes. 
Q. Well, what are they, sir? 


A. The primarily diagnosis is obsessive-compulsive 
disorder. The second is psychlosymic (phonetic) disorder. 
Now, - 


217a 


Q. What does that mean, Doctor? 


A. Yeah. Most people are familiar with the term 
“manic [p. 53] depressive or bipolar.” Psychlosymic is 
similar, in that the individual’s moods go from being 
excited and hypomanic and elated to being depressed 
and lethargic. But the swing is not as great and the 
impairment is not as great as in bipolar disorder. Drug 
dependence, alcohol abuse - 


I also diagnosed an impulse disorder of kleptomania. 
I know Your Honor is aware of the accumulation of guns, 
ammunition. But also various other tools, metallic objects. 
And he has had an obsessive need to accumulate things 
to the point where he has shoplifted even things like nuts 
and bolts. And, again, this is a man who feels so vulner- 
able and inadequate that there’s like a void inside of him. 
The more things he can collect and accumulate the more 
he thinks he’s filling that void. But since the void is 
physiologically based it never really satisfies him. 


Q. Doctor, let me ask you this: Is there any way of 
knowing for what period of time the defendant has suf- 
fered from these disorders? 


A. Yes. 
Q. Can you tell His Honor? 


A. Yeah, not in terms of an exact date, but most of 
these kind of disorders have their roots by late childhood, 
early adolescence, and develop from there. As far as the 
obsessive - compulsive disorder and the psychlosymic 
disorder, certainly by early adulthood. 


218a 


[p. 54] I’ve already indicated that based on his own 
report the pattern of alcohol abuse began at around age 
19 or 20, and of drug abuse at age 21. 


Q. So then the fall and winter of 1994 he was suffer- 
ing from these disorders? 


A. Absolutely. 


Q. All right. Now, what synergistic effect is there on 
the ability to exercise judgment and opinion for an indi- 
vidual such as this one? 


A. Well, you’ve got — you've got two things operat- 
ing in a - or three, really. You’ve got the synergistic effect 
of the various drugs and the alcohol. You've got the 
combination with an individual who is anxious and over- 
controlled, but who is disinhibited under the effect of 
those drugs and alcohol. And you've got an individual 
who when he becomes emotional also shows interference. 
So all three factors really combine into fear of judgment 
and reasoning and impulse control. 


Q. Doctor, let’s talk about the statements that 
Charles gave to the police. Now, is there a way of your 
knowing whether Charles, as opposed to an average, 
normal human being, would be more likely, less likely, or 
as likely to be misled by someone who was questioning 
him in a controlled atmosphere, like a police station or an 
atmosphere where police were around? Can you tell His 
Honor? 


A. Yes, absolutely we can. 


[p. 55] And the basis for that is this, Your Honor. 
Anybody can tell me anything. And I’ve, you know, 
evaluated literally thousands of people that have been 


a ee 


219a 


interrogated by police. And they can tell you, you know, 
the whole range of things. I’m sure Your Honor has heard 
them. 


What I look for is a consistency between what an 
individual tells me and what I find on the testing. So 
anybody can say, “I was coerced, I was influenced.” But 
there are some people who clearly have the personality 
structure where that’s not the case. And there are others 
that do have that personality structure. 


Here we have a very anxious man. One who feels 
very vulnerable. One who feels very inadequate. And one 
who would say or do just about anything to get out of a 
frightening anxiety-arousing situation. 


Q. Even tell a lie? 


A. Tell somebody a lie if it was what he thought 
that person wanted to hear in order to stop the interroga- 
tion and let him out of the situation. 


Now, let me say, Your Honor, I don’t know what the 
truth or falsity is. That’s not my role. I’m only saying that 
he has the kind of personality of an individual who 
would do that to get out of this kind of situation. 


Q. Thank you, Doctor.

Q. Sir, isn’t it true that the reliability of your opin-

[p. 64] defendant? 


| A. I’m always asked that question. And the answer 
is no, because my opinion - and I’ve been very careful to 
- to describe it this way - is that what he tells me is 


228a 


consistent with my test findings. | don’t know whether 
he’s telling the truth or not. And I don’t use that. That's 
not for me. That's for the Judge or a jury to determine. 
And I don’t use that at all. He can tell me anything. But 
he can’t have the tests come out any way he wants them 
to. And so it’s the tests I rely on. 


Q. So your testimony is that it’s irrelevant what this 
man tells you as to whether or not it’s truthful? 


A. Ina sense, yes, because I have no way of know- 
ing what's truthful and what's not. No psychologist - a 
psychologist can talk about an individual’s general ten- 
dency to be honest and truthful versus dishonest and 
manipulative. But no psychologist can say in any one 
instance whether an individual is telling the truth or 
telling a lie. We’re all capable under certain circum- 
stances, no matter what our personality is, of telling a lie. 
And that’s why psychology has never been able to sort 
that out about a single instance. 


Based on that, people who are trained in forensic are 
basically trained and experienced to say, “You know, look 
at his thought processes as he talks to you. Look at his 
emotional responses. Look at the things that indicate how 
he [p. 65] might see himself or others. But don’t draw an 
opinion about the truth or falsity of what he tells you, 
because we can’t do that.” We have no more ability to do 
that than a lay person. 


Q. Obviously, sir, there’s no way you can tell

correct? 


A. That is correct.
